We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

\tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiation field of a solenoid.

We consider a non-relativistic problem, so that we assume that the electron velocity v satisfies the condition $v/c \ll 1$ . The Pauli equation for the wave function $\psi(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ of an electron in the field of a solenoid reads

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\pmb {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {1}{2 M} \left(\pmb {p} - \frac {e}{c} [ \pmb {\nu} \times \pmb {n} ] A (r, t)\right) ^ {2} \right. \\ \left. - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {5} \\ \end{array}
$$

where M, e and $\mu$ are the mass, charge and magnetic moment of electron, $\lambda = \pm1$ corresponds to the projection $\pm1/2$ of spin onto the z axis. Since $A(r,t)$ and $B(r,t)$ are periodic functions in t with the period $T = 2\pi/\omega$ , the wave function satisfies the periodicity condition [9, 10],

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t + T) = e ^ {i \alpha T} \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {6}
$$

Let us rewrite the Pauli equation in the form

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\boldsymbol {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {\boldsymbol {p} ^ {2}}{2 M} - \frac {e \hbar A (r , t)}{M c r} l _ {z} \right. \\ \left. + \frac {e ^ {2} A ^ {2} (r , t)}{2 M c ^ {2}} - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {7} \\ \end{array}
$$

where $l_{z} = (xp_{y} - yp_{x})/\hbar$ is the projection operator of orbital momentum onto the z axis. Let the momentum of incident electron be P. From the experimental point of view, the momenta $P \gg \hbar k$ and small scattering angles $\theta \ll 1$ are of interest, while the relationship between $q = P\theta/\hbar$ and k can be arbitrary. Under these conditions, the eikonal approximation [11] is valid and we can write the wave function in the form

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {P ^ {2} t}{2 M \hbar} + i \frac {P x}{\hbar} \right\} F (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {8}
$$

Then,

$$

  "text": "\\tag {4}\n$$\n\nThese asymptotics correspond to the radiation field of a solenoid.\n\nWe consider a non-relativistic problem, so that we assume that the electron velocity v satisfies the condition $v/c \\ll 1$ . The Pauli equation for the wave function $\\psi(\\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ of an electron in the field of a solenoid reads\n\n$$\n\\begin{array}{l} i \\hbar \\frac {\\partial \\psi (\\pmb {r} , t)}{\\partial t} = \\left[ \\frac {1}{2 M} \\left(\\pmb {p} - \\frac {e}{c} [ \\pmb {\\nu} \\times \\pmb {n} ] A (r, t)\\right) ^ {2} \\right. \\\\ \\left. - \\lambda \\mu B (r, t) \\right] \\psi (\\boldsymbol {r}, t), \\tag {5} \\\\ \\end{array}\n$$\n\nwhere M, e and $\\mu$ are the mass, charge and magnetic moment of electron, $\\lambda = \\pm1$ corresponds to the projection $\\pm1/2$ of spin onto the z axis. Since $A(r,t)$ and $B(r,t)$ are periodic functions in t with the period $T = 2\\pi/\\omega$ , the wave function satisfies the periodicity condition [9, 10],\n\n$$\n\\psi (\\boldsymbol {r}, t + T) = e ^ {i \\alpha T} \\psi (\\boldsymbol {r}, t). \\tag {6}\n$$\n\nLet us rewrite the Pauli equation in the form\n\n$$\n\\begin{array}{l} i \\hbar \\frac {\\partial \\psi (\\boldsymbol {r} , t)}{\\partial t} = \\left[ \\frac {\\boldsymbol {p} ^ {2}}{2 M} - \\frac {e \\hbar A (r , t)}{M c r} l _ {z} \\right. \\\\ \\left. + \\frac {e ^ {2} A ^ {2} (r , t)}{2 M c ^ {2}} - \\lambda \\mu B (r, t) \\right] \\psi (\\boldsymbol {r}, t), \\tag {7} \\\\ \\end{array}\n$$\n\nwhere $l_{z} = (xp_{y} - yp_{x})/\\hbar$ is the projection operator of orbital momentum onto the z axis. Let the momentum of incident electron be P. From the experimental point of view, the momenta $P \\gg \\hbar k$ and small scattering angles $\\theta \\ll 1$ are of interest, while the relationship between $q = P\\theta/\\hbar$ and k can be arbitrary. Under these conditions, the eikonal approximation [11] is valid and we can write the wave function in the form\n\n$$\n\\psi (\\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \\exp \\left\\{- i \\frac {P ^ {2} t}{2 M \\hbar} + i \\frac {P x}{\\hbar} \\right\\} F (\\boldsymbol {r}, t). \\tag {8}\n$$\n\nThen,\n\n$$\ni ",

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , t) = \\frac {P}{2 \\pi \\hbar} \\left| \\int_ {- \\infty} ^ {+ \\infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \\left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \\Xi (y, t ^ {\\prime})} \\right] \\right| ^ {2} d \\theta , \\tag {12}\n$$\n\nMaking the substitutions of variables $|q|y \\to y$ , $|q|z \\to z$ and $\\omega t' \\to \\tau$ , we get\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , \\tau) = \\frac {\\hbar}{2 \\pi P} \\left| \\int_ {- \\infty} ^ {+ \\infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \\left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \\Xi (y, \\tau)} \\right] \\right| ^ {2} \\frac {d \\theta}{\\theta^ {2}},\n$$\n\n$$\n\\Xi (y, \\tau) = \\frac {\\zeta y Q}{2} \\int_ {- \\infty} ^ {\\infty} \\frac {d z}{r} \\left[ J _ {1} (\\zeta r) \\sin (\\phi) - N _ {1} (\\zeta r) \\cos (\\phi) \\right],\n$$\n\n$$\n\\zeta = \\frac {k}{| q |} = \\frac {\\hbar k}{P \\theta}, Q = \\frac {e \\Phi_ {0}}{2 c \\hbar}, \\phi = \\tau + \\frac {c}{v} \\zeta z,\n$$\n\n$$\nr = \\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \\quad s = \\operatorname{sgn} q. \\tag {13",

stage=initial_expandedrank=4query_text=For kr >> 1, what exact relation holds between the scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) in the asymptotics given?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:7point_id=faeb3a1b-8b57-5845-b0bb-273e68b8e3d2doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=1chunk_index=7modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.5584score_vector=0.6047score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0164rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.5584score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

\tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiation field of a solenoid.

We consider a non-relativistic problem, so that we assume that the electron velocity v satisfies the condition $v/c \ll 1$ . The Pauli equation for the wave function $\psi(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ of an electron in the field of a solenoid reads

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\pmb {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {1}{2 M} \left(\pmb {p} - \frac {e}{c} [ \pmb {\nu} \times \pmb {n} ] A (r, t)\right) ^ {2} \right. \\ \left. - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {5} \\ \end{array}
$$

where M, e and $\mu$ are the mass, charge and magnetic moment of electron, $\lambda = \pm1$ corresponds to the projection $\pm1/2$ of spin onto the z axis. Since $A(r,t)$ and $B(r,t)$ are periodic functions in t with the period $T = 2\pi/\omega$ , the wave function satisfies the periodicity condition [9, 10],

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t + T) = e ^ {i \alpha T} \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {6}
$$

Let us rewrite the Pauli equation in the form

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\boldsymbol {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {\boldsymbol {p} ^ {2}}{2 M} - \frac {e \hbar A (r , t)}{M c r} l _ {z} \right. \\ \left. + \frac {e ^ {2} A ^ {2} (r , t)}{2 M c ^ {2}} - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {7} \\ \end{array}
$$

where $l_{z} = (xp_{y} - yp_{x})/\hbar$ is the projection operator of orbital momentum onto the z axis. Let the momentum of incident electron be P. From the experimental point of view, the momenta $P \gg \hbar k$ and small scattering angles $\theta \ll 1$ are of interest, while the relationship between $q = P\theta/\hbar$ and k can be arbitrary. Under these conditions, the eikonal approximation [11] is valid and we can write the wave function in the form

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {P ^ {2} t}{2 M \hbar} + i \frac {P x}{\hbar} \right\} F (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {8}
$$

Then,

$$
i

\tag {17}

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

# III. CONCLUSION

The non-stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect is investigated in the non-relativistic approximation. Using the eikonal approach, the differential and total scattering cross sections are found. In contrast to the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect, the total cross section in the non-stationary case is finite. The main contribution to the scattering cross section comes from the angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} = \hbar\omega/E \ll 1$ . The differential scattering cross section contains an asymmetry with respect to the replacement $q \rightarrow -q$ . This asymmetry has a maximum at $\theta \sim \theta_{0}$ and decreases rapidly at $\theta \gg \theta_{0}$ and $\theta \ll \theta_{0}$ . The asymmetry in the cross section can be observed as the non-stationary Hall effect, and the total scattering cross section manifests itself in oscillations of the total electron flux.

stage=rerankrank=1query_text=For kr >> 1, what exact relation holds between the scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) in the asymptotics given?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:7point_id=faeb3a1b-8b57-5845-b0bb-273e68b8e3d2doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=1chunk_index=7modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.6273score_vector=0.6047score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0164rerank_score=0.8919score_composite=0.6273score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

\tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiation field of a solenoid.

We consider a non-relativistic problem, so that we assume that the electron velocity v satisfies the condition $v/c \ll 1$ . The Pauli equation for the wave function $\psi(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ of an electron in the field of a solenoid reads

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\pmb {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {1}{2 M} \left(\pmb {p} - \frac {e}{c} [ \pmb {\nu} \times \pmb {n} ] A (r, t)\right) ^ {2} \right. \\ \left. - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {5} \\ \end{array}
$$

where M, e and $\mu$ are the mass, charge and magnetic moment of electron, $\lambda = \pm1$ corresponds to the projection $\pm1/2$ of spin onto the z axis. Since $A(r,t)$ and $B(r,t)$ are periodic functions in t with the period $T = 2\pi/\omega$ , the wave function satisfies the periodicity condition [9, 10],

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t + T) = e ^ {i \alpha T} \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {6}
$$

Let us rewrite the Pauli equation in the form

$$
\begin{array}{l} i \hbar \frac {\partial \psi (\boldsymbol {r} , t)}{\partial t} = \left[ \frac {\boldsymbol {p} ^ {2}}{2 M} - \frac {e \hbar A (r , t)}{M c r} l _ {z} \right. \\ \left. + \frac {e ^ {2} A ^ {2} (r , t)}{2 M c ^ {2}} - \lambda \mu B (r, t) \right] \psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t), \tag {7} \\ \end{array}
$$

where $l_{z} = (xp_{y} - yp_{x})/\hbar$ is the projection operator of orbital momentum onto the z axis. Let the momentum of incident electron be P. From the experimental point of view, the momenta $P \gg \hbar k$ and small scattering angles $\theta \ll 1$ are of interest, while the relationship between $q = P\theta/\hbar$ and k can be arbitrary. Under these conditions, the eikonal approximation [11] is valid and we can write the wave function in the form

$$
\psi (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {P ^ {2} t}{2 M \hbar} + i \frac {P x}{\hbar} \right\} F (\boldsymbol {r}, t). \tag {8}
$$

Then,

$$
i

stage=agent_chunk_gradingrank=1query_text=For kr >> 1, what exact relation holds between the scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) in the asymptotics given?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:7point_id=faeb3a1b-8b57-5845-b0bb-273e68b8e3d2doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=1chunk_index=7modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.7373score_vector=0.6047score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0164rerank_score=0.8919score_composite=0.7373score_policy=agent_chunk_related_context_delta_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=0.7200agent_relevance_label=relevantagent_relevance_keep=Trueagent_relevance_drop=Falseagent_label_score_delta=0.03agent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=0.02agent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=Defines scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) exactly, but not the kr >> 1 asymptotic relation asked for.
We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

stage=evidence_gaterank=1query_text=For kr >> 1, what exact relation holds between the scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) in the asymptotics given?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:7point_id=faeb3a1b-8b57-5845-b0bb-273e68b8e3d2doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=1chunk_index=7modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.7373score_vector=0.6047score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0164rerank_score=0.8919score_composite=0.7373score_policy=agent_chunk_related_context_delta_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=0.7200agent_relevance_label=relevantagent_relevance_keep=Trueagent_relevance_drop=Falseagent_label_score_delta=0.03agent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=0.02agent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=Defines scalar fields E(r,t) and B(r,t) exactly, but not the kr >> 1 asymptotic relation asked for.
We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio

Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\\boldsymbol{A}(\\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\\boldsymbol{B}(\\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\\boldsymbol{E}(\\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,\n\n$$\n\\boldsymbol {A} (\\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \\boldsymbol {\\nu} \\times \\boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \\boldsymbol {B} (\\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \\boldsymbol {\\nu} B (r, t),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\boldsymbol {E} (\\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \\boldsymbol {\\nu} \\times \\boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),\n$$\n\n$$\nA (r, t) = \\Phi_ {0} \\frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \\sin (\\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \\cos (\\omega t) ],\n$$\n\n$$\nB (r, t) = \\Phi_ {0} \\frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \\sin (\\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \\cos (\\omega t) ],\n$$\n\n$$\nE (r, t) = - \\Phi_ {0} \\frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \\cos (\\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \\sin (\\omega t) ], \\tag {2}\n$$\n\nwhere $\\pmb{\\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\\pmb{n} = \\pmb{r}/r$ , $\\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \\sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \\omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.\n\nFor $kr \\ll 1$ we have\n\n$$\nA (r, t) = \\frac {\\Phi_ {0}}{2 \\pi r} \\cos (\\omega t),\n$$\n\n$$\nB (r, t) = k ^ {2} \\frac {\\Phi_ {0}}{2 \\pi} \\ln (k r) \\cos (\\omega t),\n$$\n\n$$\nE (r, t) = k \\frac {\\Phi_ {0}}{2 \\pi r} \\sin (\\omega t). \\tag {3}\n$$\n\nFor $kr \\gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are\n\n$$\nA (r, t) = \\Phi_ {0} \\sqrt {\\frac {k}{8 \\pi r}} \\cos (\\omega t - k r + \\pi / 4),\n$$\n\n$$\nB (r, t) = - \\Phi_ {0} k \\sqrt {\\frac {k}{8 \\pi r}} \\sin (\\omega t - k r + \\pi / 4),\n$$\n\n$$\nE (r, t) = k \\Phi_ {0} \\sqrt {\\frac {k}{8 \\pi r}} \\sin (\\omega t - k r + \\pi / 4). \\tag {4}\n$$\n\nThese asymptotics correspond to the radiatio",

We denote

$$
\Phi_ {0} = \frac {4 \pi^ {2} a ^ {2}}{c} n I _ {0}, \tag {1}
$$

where c is the speed of light. At $\omega = 0$ this quantity coincides with the magnetic field flux through the solenoid. We are interested in the limit $a \rightarrow 0$ at a fixed value of $\Phi_{0}$ . Solving Maxwell equation, we find the vector-potential $\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ , the magnetic field $\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ and the electric field $\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r}, t)$ outside the solenoid,

$$
\boldsymbol {A} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] A (r, t), \boldsymbol {B} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = \boldsymbol {\nu} B (r, t),
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {E} (\boldsymbol {r}, t) = [ \boldsymbol {\nu} \times \boldsymbol {n} ] E (r, t),
$$

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) - N _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
B (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ - J _ {0} (k r) \sin (\omega t) + N _ {0} (k r) \cos (\omega t) ],
$$

$$
E (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} \frac {k ^ {2}}{4} [ J _ {1} (k r) \cos (\omega t) + N _ {1} (k r) \sin (\omega t) ], \tag {2}
$$

where $\pmb{\nu}$ is a unit vector directed along the z-axis and parallel to the solenoid axis, $\pmb{n} = \pmb{r}/r$ , $\pmb{r} = (x, y, 0)$ , $r = \sqrt{x^{2} + y^{2}}$ , $k = \omega/c$ , $J_{l}(x)$ is the Bessel function, $N_{l}(x)$ is the Neumann function.

For $kr \ll 1$ we have

$$
A (r, t) = \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = k ^ {2} \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi} \ln (k r) \cos (\omega t),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \frac {\Phi_ {0}}{2 \pi r} \sin (\omega t). \tag {3}
$$

For $kr \gg 1$ the asymptotics of fields are

$$
A (r, t) = \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \cos (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
B (r, t) = - \Phi_ {0} k \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4),
$$

$$
E (r, t) = k \Phi_ {0} \sqrt {\frac {k}{8 \pi r}} \sin (\omega t - k r + \pi / 4). \tag {4}
$$

These asymptotics correspond to the radiatio
